relatch lines and record carry agent-director's CONFLICT description, the kill-failure alert's texts (`src/kill-failure-alert.ts`), which quote agent-director's kill-failure descriptions, the `ErrInvalidFlags` hold (`src/invalid-flags-hold.ts`), which posts an operator alert and logs versions agent-director reported, the persona routing, whose lines carry Slack failure text and its lost-message row read's agent-director failure text, and the stored-choice store (`src/channel-delivery.ts`), which records each persona's declaration, a credentials file's path among it, and logs its read and write failures), imports one of the helpers in `HELPER_SURFACES` (`fakeToken`, `makeCredentials`, `writeCredentialsFile`, `LEAK_SENTINEL`, `sentinelInMessage`, `sentinelTicketUrl`, `writeConfigFile`, `writeAgentDirectorConfig`, `writeChannelDeliveryRecord`, `makeReloadHarness`, `makeConnectionHarness`, `makeRoutingHarness`, `makeRecoveryHarness`, `makeStubSlackFactory`), or sets a Slack stub's `leakMarker`.
- **What a covered suite must do.** Import `assertNoLeak` from `tests/test-helpers/credentials.ts` and call it over its captured logs, errors, written files and tool results, or the audit fails naming the suite and why it counts as touching. A suite that builds `makeReloadHarness` must pass a run's `captured()` to it.
- **Exemptions.** A suite that matches but has no secret-bearing surface (nothing it logs, throws, returns or writes can hold a token) goes in the audit's `EXEMPT` map with a one-line reason. An exemption with no reason, for a missing file, for a suite that no longer touches, or for one that now calls `assertNoLeak` fails as stale; remove it. The named legs (`config`, `persona-connections`, `reload`, `reload-apply`) can't be exempted.
- **Literals.** No file under `tests/`, helpers, fixtures and scripts included, may hold a token-like value, and `LEAK_SENTINEL`'s value may appear only in the credentials helper. Build near-miss strings from the prefix constants (for example `BOT_TOKEN_PREFIX.slice(0, 3)`) as `tests/persona-identity.test.ts` does.
- **A new source module** that reads config or credentials, builds Slack clients or runs reload, or whose lines or kept state carry agent-director or Slack failure text, belongs in `SOURCE_SURFACES`, with a reason; an entry with no reason, or one matching no module under `src/`, fails as stale. To pin which imports of a module count, feed import snippets to the audit's own `touchReasonsOf(file, code)`, as the conflict latch's cases do; a new helper that builds tokens, credentials or config files belongs in `HELPER_SURFACES`. `src/live-row-sequence.ts` is one such surface (its step lines carry described agent-director failure text, and its kill results keep kill-failure descriptions raw): its functions touch, its constants and types alone do not, and every suite that touches it, `tests/live-row-sequence.test.ts` among them, calls `assertNoLeak` with no exemption. The stored-choice store is another (b.deo SRI-907): a suite touches through it when it has a value import from `src/channel-delivery.ts` that is not a SCREAMING_CASE constant (a function, renamed or not, a value namespace or a dynamic `import()`), or imports `writeChannelDeliveryRecord`, the one stored-choice helper export in `HELPER_SURFACES` (the helper's `readChannelDeliveryRecord`, `declarationOf` and `SAMPLE_SET_AT` do not touch). Every such suite, `tests/channel-delivery.test.ts` among them, calls `assertNoLeak` with no exemption. `tests/server-startup-wiring.test.ts` imports the module only as a type (`import type * as …`), so it does not touch.
- **Named legs.** `config` must reject a persona entry holding a fake token under `bot_token` and under `app_token` and one with a computed, token-named key; `persona-connections` must assert `attachOriginalToWebAPIRequestError` false on a socket client's `clientOptions` and on a Web API client's own options, drive `createUnhandledRejectionHandler` and keep its `rejected Web API calls` block; `reload` and `reload-apply` must leak-check a run's `captured()`. Renaming those rows or blocks fails the audit.
- **Source audit.** Every log call under `src/` (a console method, or `log`, `logFailure`, `logViaDeps`, `logDeps` (the click handler's log sink), `safeLog` (the persona episodes' guarded log), `recordStartupError` or `fatal`, bare or on an object) is checked for raw error text: a caught error passed as an argument, interpolated, read for its `message` or `stack`, or handed to a function that is not a safe describer (`String(err)`), an `errDescription` not through `redactSlackLogText`, or an `errName` not checked by `isSafeIdentifier`. A message is allowed only inside a safe describer call: every describer call, its arguments included, is replaced by an empty string literal before the rules run (a placeholder no name can match, so a local that shares a placeholder's name cannot taint the described calls), so `redactSlackLogText(err.message)` or `describeLogMessage(err.message)` passes while a bare `err.message` beside it is flagged. A caught error is a `catch` or `.catch(…)` binding, a name like `e`, `err`, `error` or `<word>Err`, or a name set from a raw use of one. The safe describers are listed in `SAFE_DESCRIBERS` (`describeThrownValue`, `describeThrownValueWithoutStack`, `describeLogMessage`, `describeSlackCallFailure`, `describeAgentDirectorFailure`, `describeReportedAdFailure`, `describeAdFailureForLog`, `describeCliFailure`, `describeRefreshFailure`, `redactSlackLogText`, `isSafeIdentifier`, `slackPlatformReason`, `errnoSuffix`, and `launchTimeoutFormOf`, which returns only one of CSCB's fixed launch-timeout form constants or `undefined`, never error text), each of which must still be declared under `src/`; a local sink that passes its error only through one is a describing wrapper and not itself a sink. The rule table is pinned by snippet checks, positive and negative.
- **The allow-list.** A deliberate exception (a path that is not a Slack or agent-director one, or text CSCB wrote; see the engineering guide's Error Handling) goes in `RAW_ERROR_ALLOWED` as a file, an anchor (a stable fragment of the call's text, never a line number) and a reason. An entry with no reason, for a missing file or matching no flagged call fails as stale. Fix a new finding by describing the error, not by adding an entry, unless the path really is one of those exceptions.

### Slack Stub

New persona and connection suites fake Slack with the shared helper `tests/test-helpers/slack-stub.ts`, not with hand-rolled WebClient or SocketModeClient stubs. `makeStubSlack(opts?)` builds one independent fake Slack for one persona. It has no module-scope state, network, file system, environment access or timers, and its error shapes follow the installed `@slack/web-api` and `@slack/socket-mode` libraries.

| Member | What it gives a test |
|--------|----------------------|
| `identity` | The bot user ID, bot ID and team ID a successful `auth.test` returns. The bot user ID and bot ID default to random values, distinct per stub; the team ID defaults to a fixed value. Override them through `botUserId`, `botId` and `teamId`. |
| `script` | Outcome queues `authTest`, `connect` (socket `start()`), `post` (`chat.postMessage`), `update` (`chat.update`), `upload` (`filesUploadV2`), `history` (`conversations.history`), `replies` (`conversations.replies`), `info` (`conversations.info`), `open` (`conversations.open`), `reactionsAdd`, `reactionsRemove`, `usersInfo` and `apiCall` (the generic `apiCall(method, options)`, whatever the method). Seed them through the options of the same names (every queue but `open`), or push onto them at any time; a test scripts `conversations.open` by pushing onto `script.open`. Each call takes the next outcome; an empty queue means success. |
| `calls` | Capture arrays of call arguments: `authTest`, `postMessage`, `update`, `reactionsAdd`, `reactionsRemove`, `conversationsOpen`, `usersInfo`, `filesUploadV2`, `conversationsHistory`, `conversationsReplies`, `conversationsInfo`, `apiCall` (each `{ method, options }`). Every Web API client of the stub shares them. |
| `callLog` | Every Web API call in call order, as `StubWebCall` entries (`method`, a `StubWebMethod` such as `'conversations.open'`, and `args` exactly as passed). A call is logged when it is made, so a failed call is logged too. The stub's `callLog` holds the calls of all its Web API clients; each client's `callLog` holds only its own. Assert an order with `callLog.map((c) => c.method)`, for example `['conversations.open', 'chat.postMessage']`, and no call at all with `toEqual([])`. |
| `web` | A ready Web API client with default options, for direct calls. Its bot token is the `token` option; the default is a sentinel-bearing fake token with a random suffix, distinct per stub. |
| `createWebClient(token?, options?)` | Builds a Web API client as the real constructor would; recorded in `options.web`. Without `token` it uses a fixed sentinel-bearing fake token, the same for every stub. |
| `createSocketClient(options?)` | Builds a socket client; recorded in `options.socket` and `sockets`. Throws on an empty app token, as the real constructor does. |
| `apiCall`, `filesUploadV2` | As on the real `WebClient`, these two live on a shared prototype and need the client as `this`; a detached call throws a `TypeError`, so a wrapper that loses `this` fails in tests as it would against Slack. A wrapper whose prototype is the client (the Web API watch's) reaches them through the prototype chain. The namespace methods (`chat.postMessage`, …) are bound per client and work detached. |
| `token`, `hasToken(expected)` | Every Web API client carries the bot token it was built with as `token`, like `WebClient.token`. It is not enumerable, so printing or comparing a client never shows it; assert with `hasToken(expected)`, which prints no token on failure. |
| `options` | The options every client was built with, in build order (`web`, `socket`). |
| `sockets`, `socket` | Every socket client built, and the latest one (`socket` throws if none was built). |

**Opening a DM.** An unscripted `conversations.open` succeeds with a `D…` ID derived from the requested `users`: `stubOpenedDmId(users)` returns `D0OPN` followed by the user IDs without their `U`/`W` prefix (`U0BOB` gives `D0OPN0BOB`), so each user gets its own ID and none equals the event factories' default DM (`D0STUB0001`). Use `stubOpenedDmId` to compute the ID a test expects the post to go to. To choose the ID, push `openedDm(id)` onto `script.open`; to fail the open, push a `platform` outcome such as `missing_scope`, `user_not_found` or `cannot_dm_bot`.

**Passing a stub client as a `WebClient`.** `WebClient` is a class with private members, so no plain object satisfies it. Where code under test takes a `WebClient` (a persona's client in the registry's `SessionToolDeps.clientFor`, the connection manager's factory), pass `asWebClient(client)`, the helper's one cast, rather than casting in the test file. `makeStubSlackFactory()` already uses it.

The socket stub behaves like `SocketModeClient` with auto-reconnect off:

- `start()` takes the next `connect` outcome and first runs the connect leg (`apps.connections.open`). A failure there (the Web API kinds, `no-url` or `reject`) rejects `start()` with no lifecycle event.
- Otherwise `start()` emits `authenticated` and `connecting`, and the WebSocket phase settles on a later microtask according to the outcome. `startCalls` counts calls, and `start()` can be called again after a drop, so a test can drive a reopen.
- `drop()` is Slack closing the connection, the case the connection manager reopens: it emits `close` and `disconnected`, and a `start()` still waiting for `hello` rejects with no value. `disconnect()` is the client's own close, which is not reopened: it is counted in `disconnectCalls` and emits `disconnecting`, then `close` (if a WebSocket phase ever began) and `disconnected`.
- `deliver(event, envelope?)` sends an Events API event and `deliverInteractive(payload)` an interactive payload, in the shape `server.ts` consumes. Both need a connected socket and resolve once every listener settles.
- The envelope (`body`) `deliver` sends carries `is_ext_shared_channel: false` by default, as Slack's does, and the per-type and `slack_event` listeners get the same envelope (b.deo SRI-1203). `envelope` overrides (`EnvelopeOverrides`) are merged over that default; a key set to `undefined` is removed. `ENVELOPE_FLAG_FORMS` holds the flag's four forms (`EnvelopeFlagForm`: `false`, `true`, `absent`, and `non-boolean`, the string `'false'`) as overrides, and `envelopeFlagOf(envelope?)` is the flag value an envelope with those overrides carries. The flag is never put on the event.
- `acks` records each ack in order; `lifecycle` records every lifecycle event emitted, in order.

**Scripted outcomes.** Each queue entry is one outcome kind:

| Kind | Result |
|------|--------|
| `ok` | Success. On `auth.test`, `post`, `update`, `upload`, `history`, `replies`, `info` and `open`, an optional `result` is merged over the default response; a key set to `undefined` is removed. |
| `platform` | A named Slack error (an `ok: false` answer) with that `error`, and an optional `retryAfter` in the response metadata. |
| `network`, `dns`, `timeout`, `http`, `rate-limited` | Slack unreachable: a request error, an HTTP error with a status, or a rate-limited error with `retryAfter` in seconds. |
| `no-url` | Connect only: `apps.connections.open` answered without a URL. `start()` rejects with a plain error on the connect leg, with no lifecycle event. |
| `closed-before-hello`, `websocket-error` | Connect only: the WebSocket closes before `hello`. `start()` rejects with no value after `close` and `disconnected`; `websocket-error` emits `error` first. |
| `never` | Connect: `apps.connections.open` answers, `authenticated` and `connecting` are emitted, and the WebSocket phase never reaches `hello`; `start()` ends only on `drop()` or `disconnect()`. Web API calls: the call never settles. |
| `open-never-answers` | Connect only: `apps.connections.open` never answers. `start()` stays pending for good with no lifecycle event; `disconnect()` does not end it. |
| `deferred` | Connect: built with `makeDeferredConnect()`. `start()` waits at the connect leg until the test calls `settle(outcome?)` (default `ok`), then proceeds as that outcome. Settling after the manager abandoned the `start()` drives a late settlement. Web API calls: built with `makeDeferredWebApiCall()`; push its `outcome` onto any Web API queue (`script.open`, `script.post`, …) and each call that takes it waits until `settle(outcome?)` (default `ok`, any kind but `deferred`), then answers as that outcome. Use it to hold an open or post in flight, for example to show concurrent posts share one open or that an attempt settling after `cancel` changes nothing. `settled` says whether it was settled; a second `settle` throws. |
| `reject` | The call rejects with the given value exactly as given (a plain error, a string, `undefined` and so on). |

Set `leakMarker` to `LEAK_SENTINEL` and every error the stub builds carries the marker wherever a real error can hold secrets: the message, the wrapped `original` error and its headers, HTTP headers and body, fields of `data`, and the error the socket's `error` event carries. In a message (the error's own, the `original`'s, the `error` event's, the `apps.connections.open` failure's) the marker sits only inside a fake token and a `wss://…?ticket=` URL, since log lines keep a message after `redactSlackLogText`; everywhere else it is bare, since CSCB must never read those fields. `data.error` stays exactly as scripted, so classification still sees the real Slack error code.

Not every failure carries the marker. A rejection with no value carries nothing: `closed-before-hello`, and the `start()` rejection after `websocket-error`. `reject` passes its value unchanged, so plant the sentinel in the value if the row needs it. Run `assertNoLeak` over what the code under test produced (see Credentials Fixtures and Leak Checks).

**Event factories.** `makeChannelMessage`, `makePrivateChannelMessage`, `makeGroupDmMessage`, `makeDm`, `makeBotMessage`, `makeWebhookPost` and `makeAppMention` build Slack events with defaults and overrides (a key set to `undefined` is removed). `makePrivateChannelMessage` is a private-channel message (`channel_type` `group`, channel `G0STUB0001`) and `makeGroupDmMessage` a group-DM message (`channel_type` `mpim`, channel `G0STUBMPIM`), so the two never share an ID (b.deo SRI-1203). A group-DM `app_mention` is `makeAppMention` with a `G…` channel; it carries no `channel_type`. Text builders for message bodies:

- `mentionText(userId, label?)`: a user mention, `<@U…>`, or `<@U…|label>` with a label. Pass the stub's `identity.botUserId` to mention that stub's bot.
- `broadcastText(kind?, label?)`: `<!here>` (the default), `<!channel>` or `<!everyone>`, or the labelled form such as `<!here|here>`.
- `userGroupMentionText(groupId?, label?)`: a user-group mention, `<!subteam^S…>`, or the labelled form, with a fixed default group ID. It mentions no persona; use it to prove that a user-group mention does not deliver.

**Reference example.** The `Slack validation classification (both legs, sentinel-bearing errors)` describe block in `tests/persona-connections.test.ts` is a table test. Each row scripts one outcome and runs it against a fresh sentinel-bearing stub on each leg it applies to: `auth.test`, `socket-mode` or both. It checks the class, the reason fields and `assertNoLeak` on everything captured.

### Slack Library Log Probes

Tests of what the Slack libraries print (the socket logger's list of forwarded lines, the redacting Web API logger, the production factory's real clients) use `tests/test-helpers/slack-logger-probes.ts`, shared by `tests/persona-connections.test.ts` and `tests/persona-connection-wiring.test.ts`. Don't redefine these probes in a suite.

| Export | Use it for |
|--------|------------|
| `ticketUrl(origin)` | A WebSocket URL as `apps.connections.open` returns it, its connection ticket carrying `LEAK_SENTINEL`, so any line that prints it fails `assertNoLeak`. |
| `PONG_TIMEOUT_TEXT` | The library's pong-timeout warning, one of the connection-health lines the socket logger forwards. |
| `socketLogLine(persona, text)` | The exact `[slack] persona Socket Mode:` line a persona's socket logger writes for `text`. |
| `outputDuring(fn)`, `outputDuringAsync(fn)` | Every call the console methods, `process.stdout.write` and `process.stderr.write` received while `fn` ran (or until its promise settled), all silenced and then restored, a suite's own spy included. Pass the result to `assertNoLeak` before any assertion that could print it. |

### Fake Clock

Persona suites that need virtual time use the shared helper `tests/test-helpers/fake-clock.ts`, not hand-rolled single-shot timer fakes. The connection manager takes its clock and timers by injection, so tests pass it a clock from `createFakeClock` rather than using Bun's global fake timers. The helper also flushes pending promise continuations after each timer it fires and lets a test list the pending timers, which is what driving the backoff, the 10 s bounds on an `auth.test` or `start()` that never settles, and the bring-up and reopen retry timers without sleeping needs.

`createFakeClock({ start?, flushTurns? })` builds one independent clock. Its `now`, `setTimeout` and `clearTimeout` satisfy the connection manager's `PersonaConnectionClock`, so a test passes the clock with no cast. Virtual time moves only when the test moves it.

| Member | What it gives a test |
|--------|----------------------|
| `now()` | The current virtual time in ms (default start 0). |
| `advance(ms)`, `advanceTo(time)` | Move virtual time forward, firing every due timer in due-time order, including timers that callbacks schedule during the move. Resolves with the number fired. |
| `runNext()` | Jump to the earliest pending timer and fire every timer due then. |
| `flush()` | Let pending promise continuations run without moving time. The clock does the same before, between and after firings, so a stub's settled promise chain finishes before the next timer fires. |
| `pending()`, `pendingCount()`, `firedCount()` | Introspection: each pending timer's requested `delayMs`, `dueAt` and `scheduledAt`, earliest first; the pending count; callbacks fired so far. |

`createPersonaEpisodes` takes the same clock shape as its optional `clock` (`PersonaEpisodesClock`, absent the system clock): pass a fake clock and move it with `advance` to check an episode's `startedAt`; production passes none, which `server-startup-wiring.test.ts` pins. The `tmux-unresponsive` condition's alert check (b.jg5 SRJ-309) is a timer on that same clock, so a test drives it with `advance` and sees it in `pending()`; the recovery harness passes `alertThresholdMs: false` where a case wants the retry timer as the only timer.

Code that only reads the time takes a plain `() => number` clock (the durable queue's `now` option); pass the fake clock's `now` and move time with `advance`, never `Date.now` or a hand-written counter.

Code that reads the time through a module seam takes the fake clock's `now` there: the stale-`working`-row wait and evidence in `src/session-manager.ts` (`_setNow`, reset with `_resetNow` in `afterEach`; bug b.f2b). Move it with `await clock.advance(ms)`, between reconnect attempts or from the agent-director stub's status poll, one step per poll; the wait's poll interval is still a real timer (1 ms in these cases). Each launch call's window (b.jg5 SRJ-407; `launchCallWindowOf`) reads the same `_now`: on the recovery harness build with `harnessNow: true` and move the clock inside the call through the stub's `spawnFn` / `resumeFn` (or `scriptTimedLaunch`), so the window is `{ startMs: t0, endMs: t0 + takesMs }`; place a row's launch start on both bounds and 1 ms outside each, never a literal date. The stub's `SAMPLE_LAUNCH_START_DEFAULT` lies outside any window near fake time 0, so a case gets no "this launch's row" by accident. The `findMissing` memo's window reads the same `_now` (b.jg5 SRJ-120): drive it with `createFakeClock` passed through `_setNow` and step to the edge with the exported `FIND_MISSING_MEMO_TTL_MS` (a reuse at `FIND_MISSING_MEMO_TTL_MS - 1` ms after the run's call resolved, a new call at the window's end), never a literal 10 s. Never freeze `Date.now()` with Bun's `setSystemTime` for this.


The bounded kill retry (`src/kill-retry.ts`, b.jg5 SRJ-702) takes its wait by injection: a `KillRetryClock` (`setTimeout` only, which `createFakeClock()` satisfies) or a sleep function. Pass the fake clock and drive it with `runNext()` or `advance(KILL_RETRY_SPACING_MS)`, or through the recovery harness's `drive` (see Backoff and Retry Tests).

The latch re-check's timers (`createLatchRecheckController`, `src/conflict-latch.ts`, b.jg5 SRJ-505) take their clock by injection (`LatchRecheckClock`: `setTimeout` and `clearTimeout`, which `createFakeClock()` satisfies). Drive the 120 s interval (`LATCH_RECHECK_INTERVAL_MS`) with the fake clock's `advance`, or on the recovery harness with `advanceToRecheck()` (opt in with `latchRecheck: true`), never with a hand-written timer.

The live-row sequence (`src/live-row-sequence.ts`) takes its clock in its dependencies (`LiveRowSequenceDeps.clock`, a `NeverEarlyWaitClock`, which `createFakeClock()` satisfies); on the recovery harness it is the harness clock with each sequence timer tracked, driven by `driveSequence` (see Backoff and Retry Tests).

A zero, negative or overlong delay fires after 1 ms, as in Bun, but `pending()` still reports the requested `delayMs`. Assert on the requested delay to check what the code asked for.

A derived wait (G, the alert threshold or B) that can be longer than the timer maximum (`MAX_TIMER_DELAY_MS`) is armed with `armNeverEarlyWait` from `src/ad-settings.ts` (a wait on a `pending` row through `armPendingRowWait`, `src/pending-row.ts`, which arms it), never a plain `setTimeout`; its test drives the chain with `runNext()`, asserting before each step that at most one timer is pending and none asks more than the maximum, then that the callback ran once exactly at the deadline. A wait that must follow the values in effect is armed with a getter (such as `adLaunchBoundMsInEffect`) rather than a number, so the helper reads it again at every timer fire; its test changes the value while the wait is armed and asserts that a raised value never ends the wait at the old deadline and a lowered one ends it at the next fire or exactly at the new deadline. The pending-row rule arms no wait of its own: it compares the row's age at each retry (`pendingRowAgeOf`), so its cases move the harness clock to G or B past the launch start (`pastSampleGrace(h)` for G) and fire each retry with `retryNow(h, key)`, whose waits double from 30 s to 300 s; its beyond-maximum case sets a G above `MAX_TIMER_DELAY_MS` (and one at `AD_WAIT_NEVER_ENDS`) through the settings and asserts that retries well past the default G and B make no lap, run or post while the timer keeps running; `pendingRowAgeOf` itself compares such a G at each call and never arms it.

### Backoff and Retry Tests

Backoff, retry and timeout tests never wait in real time.

- **Pure schedules are called, not timed.** `createPersonaRetrySchedule` holds no timer and reads no clock: it returns the next wait. Tests drive it by calling `nextDelayMs` and asserting on the returned sequence, as the `backoff schedule` describe block does.
- **Timers and clocks are injected.** Code that schedules a wait, or abandons a call that never settles, takes its clock and timers as parameters. Tests pass the shared fake clock (see Fake Clock) and advance it; they never sleep past a real delay.
- **The UNAVAILABLE retry timer runs only on the fake clock.** `src/unavailable-retry.ts` takes its clock and timers by injection, so its tests drive it on `createFakeClock()`, bare or through `makeRecoveryHarness`, and never on a real timer; the restart module's real-timer exception (see Serialization and Teardown Tests) does not extend to it. The harness's full-mode retries call the restart module's retry entry, which arms no restart timer, so they need no real wait beyond `settle()`. The one real 0 ms `setTimeout` in its attempt-frames case (listed there) is no retry timer. Take its waits from `UNAVAILABLE_RETRY_BASE_S` and `UNAVAILABLE_RETRY_CEILING_S` through `doublingBackoffDelay`; the SRD's numbers appear only in the one pin case.
- **The bounded kill retry runs only on the fake clock (b.jg5 SRJ-702).** Its 2 s waits between tries are never real: `runKillRetry` takes its wait by injection, so `tests/kill-retry.test.ts` passes `createFakeClock()` and moves it only to the retry's own pending wait, asserting the delay the code asked for; the live-row sequence's kills take the sequence's injected clock (the recovery harness's sequence clock, through `buildLiveRowSequenceDeps`), the restart kill adapter `_buildKillSessionAdapter(getPersona, clock)`, the start sweep `reconcileOrphans(config, clock)`, the CLI teardown's kill `CliDeps.sleep` (the bundle's fake clock in `tests/cli.test.ts`; `cliKillRetry` in `tests/cli-teardown.test.ts`), and the persona teardown's kill `killPersonaInstanceForTeardown(key, { clock })` (b.jg5 SRJ-715: the recovery harness's `teardownDeps()` binds it to `h.killRetryClock`, the reload harness's real composition to `run.clock`, and `tests/persona-lifecycle.test.ts`'s real-kill fixture to its own `killClock`). On the recovery harness use `h.killRetryClock` and drive the work with `h.drive(work)`; elsewhere move a fake clock to each pending wait as it is set (`reconcileOnClock` in `tests/session-manager.test.ts`). Take the try count and spacing from `KILL_RETRY_TRIES` and `KILL_RETRY_SPACING_MS`, never literals. The harness's `cleanup()` fails while any timer, a wait between tries included, is still pending, so drive every kill's tries to their end. Why: three tries 2 s apart at every retried kill would make the suite several times slower and its timing flaky.
- **The live-row sequence runs only on the fake clock (b.jg5 SRJ-705).** Its step-2 wait, its run spacing, its step-4 pause and its kills' waits between tries all go through the clock in its dependencies, never a real timer. On the recovery harness start it with `h.runSequence(key, request)`, or `h.startSequence` and then `h.driveSequence(work)`, which move the harness clock to each pending sequence timer as it is set; the harness's `cleanup()` fails while a sequence timer is still pending. Take the pace and caps from the exported constants (`LIVE_ROW_SEQUENCE_RUN_SPACING_MS`, `LIVE_ROW_SEQUENCE_STEP4_PAUSE_MS`, `LIVE_ROW_SEQUENCE_STEP3_RUNS`, `LIVE_ROW_SEQUENCE_MAX_RUNS`, `LIVE_ROW_SEQUENCE_MAX_KILLS`), never literals, and G from `adGraceMsInEffect()`; a case with G beyond the timer maximum asserts, as for any derived wait, that at most one timer is pending, none asks more than the maximum and the first run comes exactly at the deadline. Why: one sequence waits up to G plus about 15 s, plus each kill's tries.
- **Never-settling cases.** A `never` outcome stays pending for good, so the test ends it through the stub (`drop()`, `disconnect()`) or through the injected clock, never by waiting for it.
- **`retryAfter` rows.** Script `retryAfter` values above and below the current step, and assert that the wait is never shorter than `retryAfter`. The outcome carries `retryAfter` in seconds and the schedule returns milliseconds, so convert before comparing. The `retryAfter` describe block is the reference.
- **One exception: the real spawn path.** A case that launches through the real `spawnForPersona` polls in real time for the spawn only (see Connection Wiring and Bring-Up Tests); its backoff and retry waits stay on the fake clock.
- **Leak checks still apply.** Every captured log line and error passes through `assertNoLeak`.

### Destination Failure Tests

A post to a persona's destination that fails is held and retried by the destination hold (`src/persona-destination-hold.ts`), shared by the permission poller and the notifier. Test it on a fake clock, never in real time.

- **Always inject a fake-clock hold.** Any test that can hold a notice or prompt passes a `destinationHold` built with `createPersonaDestinationHold({ destinations, getPersona, clientFor, clock: createFakeClock(), log })`. The notifier's and the poller's default hold uses the real clock, so a held notice would start a real timer. To build the resolver, the fake-clock hold and the notifier together, as `server.ts` wires them, call `makeNotifierStack({ getPersona, clientFor, getPersonaConfig, log, clock?, isDryRun?, recordStartupError? })` from `tests/test-helpers/persona-notifier.ts`; it returns `notifier`, `hold`, `destinations` and `clock`. `makeNotifierHarness` (`h.clock`, `h.hold`), `makeRoutingHarness` (`h.holdClock`, `h.hold`) and `makeManagedRouting` are built on it; `makeHold` in `tests/permission-poller.test.ts` does it for the poller.
- **Make the failure sticky.** One scripted failure is used up by the first attempt, so the first retry would succeed. To keep the cause in place across retries, fill the queue: `stub.script.open.push(...Array<WebApiOutcome>(1000).fill({ kind: 'platform', error: 'missing_scope' }))` (or `script.post` with `not_in_channel`).
- **Clear the cause.** `stub.script.open.length = 0` empties the queue, so the next call succeeds; push `openedDm(id)` after it to choose the DM.
- **Drive the retries.** `clock.pending()[0].delayMs` is the wait the hold asked for (5 000, then 10 000, doubling to 300 000); `clock.runNext()` fires the next retry and `clock.advance(ms)` moves time, for example to show nothing is attempted 1 ms before the due time. A poller prompt has no timer of its own: advance the clock past the due time, then `tick()`.
- **Assert the episode, not the attempts.** One `persona-destination-failed` start line per episode and one `cleared:` line after the cause clears, no line per failed retry, each held item posted exactly once after it clears, and `hold.view(key)` (`held`, `heldNotices`, `nextDueAt`). For a notice, `onPostFailure` runs once, at its first failed attempt.
- **Clean up.** A hold that can hold notices sets retry timers: call `hold.cancelAll()` in `afterEach`, and assert `clock.pendingCount()` is 0 once a case's held items are delivered. A hold used only by the poller sets no timer.

`tests/persona-destination-hold.test.ts` is the reference for the hold alone; the `destination failures are held and retried` describe block in `tests/persona-notifier.test.ts` and the `a destination failure holds the persona's prompts` block in `tests/permission-poller.test.ts` for the two callers.

### Connection Manager Tests

Connection manager tests build `createPersonaConnectionManager` from injected fakes only: the `factory` from `makeStubSlackFactory()` (in `tests/test-helpers/slack-stub.ts`) and the `clock` from `createFakeClock()`, which supplies both the clock and the timers. Always pass both: an omitted `factory` falls back to `PRODUCTION_SLACK_CLIENT_FACTORY` (real Slack clients) and an omitted `clock` to `SYSTEM_PERSONA_CONNECTION_CLOCK` (real timers). A test never builds a real Slack client.

| `makeStubSlackFactory()` member | What it gives a test |
|--------|----------------------|
| `factory` | The `PersonaSlackClientFactory` to pass to the manager, uncast. It routes each build to the persona whose token it received: the app token for a socket client, the bot token for the validation and long-lived Web API clients. |
| `addPersona(key, tokens, opts?)` | Registers a persona with its own `makeStubSlack(opts)` stub, as its `INITIAL_CREDENTIALS` set. No identity, script queue, capture or socket is shared with another persona. |
| `addCredentials(key, label, tokens, opts?)`, `credentials(key, label)` | Registers another credential set (a rotated pair) for a registered persona, with its own stub: its own identity, script queues, captures and sockets. Every build that receives one of its tokens goes to that stub and is recorded with `persona: key` and `credentials: label`, so a persona's old and new tokens are scripted and asserted apart. `credentials(key, label)` is that set's stub. |
| `activity`, `activityOf(key)` | What happened to every client built, in order: `built`, `started`, `connected` (reached `hello`), `disconnected` (the client's own close) and `discarded` (its last listener removed), each with its `kind`, `persona` and `credentials` label. A credentials reconnect that opens before it closes reads `connected` for the new set before `discarded` and `disconnected` for the old one. A drop by Slack is the test's own act and is not recorded. |
| `persona(key)` | That persona's `INITIAL_CREDENTIALS` stub. `socket` is its latest socket client and `sockets` every one built, abandoned ones included; clients built from a set added with `addCredentials` are on that set's stub. |
| `builds`, `buildsOf(key, kind?)` | Every client built, in build order, each with its `kind` (`socket`, `validation` or `web`), `persona`, `credentials` label and `options` exactly as received. |
| `hasToken(expected)` | On each build record: whether the client got exactly that token. The token itself is not enumerable, so a failing assertion never prints it. |

Rules:

- **Check client options through the build records.** Assert on `options` in `builds` or `buildsOf`, per client kind. A socket build's options hold the app token, so drop that field before comparing. Check tokens with `hasToken`, never by reading or printing the token.
- **Use the file-level harness.** `makeHarness()` in `tests/persona-connections.test.ts` builds personas A and B, their credentials files and stubs (leak marker on), the manager, the fake clock and the captured lines, statuses and events. Its `afterEach` stops every harness manager with `stopAll()` and runs `assertNoLeak` over everything captured. A new connection test uses it rather than building a second harness.
- **Isolation tests check both personas.** Whenever one persona fails, drops, is rejected or hangs, the test also delivers on the healthy persona (`expectDelivers`) and checks its events still arrive, tagged with that persona. Asserting only on the failing persona does not show isolation.

**Reference example.** The `AC 5: per-persona isolation walk-through (fake clock)` describe block in `tests/persona-connections.test.ts` is the model. Each case is named "AC 5: …". A's socket drops and reopens at once while B keeps delivering. A rejected reopen waits 5 s, then 10 s, and B keeps delivering throughout. A reopen whose `start()` never settles is abandoned at 10 s on the fake clock and retried. The case `AC 5: no agent-director call is made for A across a drop, a rejected reopen, an abandoned reopen and a refused reopen` counts calls on an agent-director recorder built with `makeStubClient` and asserts zero.

### Connection Wiring and Bring-Up Tests

The server's per-persona wiring lives in importable modules, so test it there, never through `main()`:

- **Connection seams and the event router.** Build personas on the real connection manager with `makeConnectionHarness(specs, baseDir, opts?)` from `tests/test-helpers/persona-connection-harness.ts`. It uses the shared stub factory and a fake clock, and builds `clientFor` and the identity lookup with the same `persona-start.ts` factories `server.ts` uses. Its `onEvent` and `onStatus` are late-bound, so a test can build the real `createPersonaEventRouter` or `createPersonaUpFlushListener` over the harness and plug it in. With `files: true` it writes each persona's credentials file and working directory under `baseDir`, so the real start procedure passes steps 1–2. The `tokens` option (by persona name, each built with `fakeToken`) registers a given token set (and, with `files: true`, writes it) instead of the default fakes, for example a rotated one. The `serialize` option hands the manager a serializer's `run`, as `server.ts` passes `personaLifecycle.run`; without it the manager gets none, so the network close of a socket detached by a refused Web API call (bug b.ujn) runs at once instead of waiting for the persona's turn. Pass it in any test about that window. `h.connections` has only `bringUp`: it records each call in `h.bringUpCalls` and appends `slack:<key>` to `h.order`, a shared step log the test may add its own markers to. Call `h.manager.stopAll()` after each test. `tests/persona-connection-wiring.test.ts` is the reference.
- **Real routing over the harness.** To drive the real `createPersonaRouting` with the manager's identities and clients, build it with `makeManagedRouting(h, baseDir, { archive? })` from `tests/test-helpers/persona-routing-managed.ts`, which wires it as `server.ts` does, except that it binds no stored-choice store (no `getChannelDelivery`, so in fungible mode no stored choice applies and a channel is decided as `mentions`), and registers a session per persona. A case that needs stored choices uses `makeRoutingHarness`'s `channelDelivery` option. Its `notify` is the real persona notifier (from `makeNotifierStack`, with `getPersonaConfig` reading the harness's configuration) over the harness's persona and client lookups, with its hold on its own fake clock, and its user-name lookup is `users.info` on the persona's own client (the user ID when it has none). It returns only `routing`, `logs` and `notifications`; for a test that asserts notices or the hold, use `makeRoutingHarness` (see Inbound delivery below). Don't hand-wire the routing deps. The caller owns `initRestart`, `resetRoutingState()` and `h.manager.stopAll()`.
- **The start procedure.** Drive the real bring-up controller, `createPersonaBringUpController`, alone (`controller.bringUp(persona, applied)`) or as `startupSessionManager`'s `bringUp`. Build it over a harness with `files: true`: `{ bringUp: h.connections.bringUp, status: (key) => h.manager.status(key), stop: (key, endReason) => h.manager.stop(key, endReason) }` (or the manager itself) as `connections`, since the controller needs `status` and `stop` (a `claude_config_dir` hold closes the persona's connection) as well as `bringUp`, `h.clock` as `clock`, an injected `checkConfigDir` (see Isolation), the `fs` seam for credentials and directory failures, and a recording `launch` (or one that calls the real `spawnForPersona`, as `server.ts` does). Compose `controller.onConnectionStatus` into `h.onStatus` with `composePersonaStatusListeners`, so a persona that reaches `up` through a retry is launched. Assert the step order, each persona's outcome (`up`, `broken` or `retrying`) through `controller.state(key)` or the start pass's `perPersona`, that a broken or retrying persona launches nothing, and, in dry run, that no credentials file is read. `tests/persona-bringup.test.ts` is the reference for the order; `makeBringUpFixture` in `tests/persona-connections.test.ts` (`bring-up outcomes (E5)`) for outcomes and retry timers. Its `dropSessions` option wires the controller's `onLeftUp` as `server.ts` does (`createNotUpSessionDropper` over the real `dropPersonaSession`), for cases where a persona that stops being up must lose its MCP session.
- **MCP admission of a not-up persona.** `server.ts`'s initialized handler can't be imported, so decide a session through the real `decideSessionAdmission` with the production up check (`createPersonaUpPredicate` over the manager and the controller) and have the test promote an admitted session or close a refused one, as that handler does. `registry.test.ts` (`not-up personas: MCP session admission and drop`) and the surviving-instance cases in `persona-connections.test.ts` are the references; a source audit in `server-startup-wiring.test.ts` pins what the handler itself does with a refusal.
- **MCP admission from a held directory (b.jg5 SRJ-810, SRJ-1505).** Drive the real `decideSessionAdmission` with a `heldDirectory` query: a stub (`registry.test.ts`'s held-directory describe) for the query's own rules (asked first, with the roots real path; a throw refusing; an answer with no id not held), or the session manager's `oldLifeHeldDirectory` over a real hold set for a case about a hold the server began (the recovery harness's `h.admitSession(rootsPath)`, the reload harness's `run.admitSession(name)` with `opts.realLaunch`). Assert the `held` answer with its directory and ids and the one `sessionHeldRefusalLine`, never a `not-up` or `unmatched` answer, and that the session is admitted once the hold ends. `server-startup-wiring.test.ts` pins that `handleInitialized` passes `oldLifeHeldDirectory`.
- **Retry timers.** Directory re-checks and Slack retries run on the harness's fake clock: advance it through the backoff steps and assert on the requested delays and the stat times, never sleep. In `afterEach`, call `controller.cancelAll()` and `h.manager.stopAll()`, then assert `h.clock.pendingCount()` is 0, so no retry timer leaks into the next test.
- **The real spawn path uses real timers.** Await its actual launch completion; use the shared fake clock only for dependencies that accept it.
- **The start pool.** Drive `startupSessionManager` with the controller as `bringUp` and launches held open, so the test can show every persona's Slack bring-up happens before any launch settles, that no more than `concurrency` launches run at once, in readiness order, that a broken or retrying persona uses no slot, and that a retrying persona that comes up later is launched outside the pool. The `startupSessionManager` bring-up cases in `tests/session-manager.test.ts` are the reference.
- **Isolation still applies.** Every persona path sits under the test's `mkdtempSync` directory, and a persona failing its bring-up is checked alongside a healthy one (see Connection Manager Tests).

A test that needs the persona key an action ID was built for uses `personaKeyFromActionId` from `tests/test-helpers/action-id-key.ts`; production takes the key from the receiving connection instead.

### Serialization and Teardown Tests

Every lifecycle operation for a persona runs through the per-persona serializer (`src/persona-serializer.ts`), and the teardown in `src/persona-lifecycle.ts` is the one path that clears a persona's state.

- **Controllable promises, never delays.** Serializer tests settle each operation from the test (a `Promise.withResolvers` gate) and check order after flushing continuations; no timer and no real delay. Elsewhere, hold a persona's turn by submitting a gated operation to the same serializer the code under test uses (`f.serializer.run(key, …)` in `makeBringUpFixture`, the fixture's serializer in `tests/persona-lifecycle.test.ts`), never a stand-in lock.
- **The accepted real-timer exceptions.** `initRestart` (`src/restart.ts`) takes no clock, so its timer is real wherever the restart module runs. The restart-module cases in `tests/persona-lifecycle.test.ts` set `getRestartDelay` to 0.001 s (a real 1 ms `setTimeout`) and wait for it with a 1 ms-step poll capped at 2 s; only those cases poll in real time, and every other case there flushes continuations or uses the fake clock. `tests/restart.test.ts` waits for its short restart timers with `Bun.sleep(WAIT_MS)` (50 ms), and its `RestartDeps.serialize` cases follow that pattern; b.d61's same-run cases (`d61Run`), the re-probe cases after `escalate-dead`, the slow-recovery observer cases and the several-tick cases, all through `awaitRuns`, instead await their restart work's end, so a timer left by another case cannot change their counts. The clean-exit check that `scheduleRestart` arms beside a full-delay restart timer (b.4vm) is real for the same reason: the b.4vm cases there shorten it with the test-only seam `_setCleanExitRelaunchDelay` (to 0.01 s or less; `_resetRestartState` restores `CLEAN_EXIT_RELAUNCH_DELAY`) and wait for it with the same `Bun.sleep(WAIT_MS)`, their full delay (60 s) never firing during a case, and the b.4vm case through the real relaunch gate waits for its launch through the real `spawnForPersona` with a 1 ms-step poll capped at 2 s. The loop guard's `session_restart_delay` window is never waited out: those cases give it a fake clock through the test-only seam `_setRestartNow` (`createFakeClock().now`, stepped with `advance` or `advanceTo`; `_resetRestartState` restores `Date.now`). That exception covers restart timers and their clean-exit checks only: its retry-entry cases (`runRestartRetry`) call the entry directly, with no timer, and wait on microtask flushes and controllable promises; only the per-key active-count cases there fire one real restart timer beside a retry, waited out with the same `Bun.sleep(WAIT_MS)`. The restart-module cases in `tests/persona-lifecycle.test.ts` also build the real UNAVAILABLE retry controller, on a fake clock whose timers never fire. The attempt-frames case in `tests/unavailable-retry.test.ts` sets one real 0 ms `setTimeout` inside an attempt and awaits its callback: it is no retry timer, and it must be real because the fake clock does not carry the attempt context into its callbacks. The launch's working-row wait sleeps its poll interval on a real timer, so the launch cases in `tests/unavailable-retry.test.ts` and `tests/tmux-unresponsive.test.ts` whose wait goes on past a failed poll (b.jg5 SRJ-605) set `agent_director_poll_interval_ms` to 1 ms (`WAIT_POLL_MS`). No health-check case runs a real interval: a tick body runs through the module's test seam `_runHealthCheckTickForTest()` (`src/health-check.ts`; the body the interval runs, with the same guards, resolving once it ends), so `tests/health-check.test.ts` drives exactly the ticks it needs (`driveTicks(n)`, `runTicks`) and the `armMissingRetryTimer` case in `tests/tmux-unresponsive.test.ts` runs one body through the file-local `healthTick`, the tick's start read from the harness's fake clock. The `startHealthCheck` cases in `tests/health-check.test.ts` catch the interval it arms with a file-local `armedIntervals(arm)`, which swaps the global `setInterval` for a recorder only while `arm` runs and hands back every interval set, none scheduled. The AC 46 cases in `tests/conflict-latch.test.ts` run no real timer for these two: a file-local `captureTimer(kind, arm)` swaps the global `setTimeout` (around `scheduleRestart`) or `setInterval` (around `startHealthCheck(1)`) for a recorder only while `arm` runs, restores the global in a `finally`, fails unless exactly one timer was set, and hands back the captured callback, which the case awaits by hand (one restart, one tick; the health check is stopped after it). Swap a timer global only that way: for the one call that arms it, never across awaits or for a whole test. Don't add a real timer anywhere else.
- **Hold notices on a fake clock.** A teardown or apply test that can hold a notice uses a destination hold on `createFakeClock()` (see Destination Failure Tests), and asserts a held notice for the removed persona is cancelled, its timer gone.

### Reload Tests

The reload controller (`src/reload.ts`) takes its paths, tick driver, Slack client factory, lifecycle operations, file writer and stored-choice store (`channelDelivery`) by injection (b.av2 SR-13.1). Test it through `makeReloadHarness(opts?)` from `tests/test-helpers/reload-harness.ts` (b.av2 SR-13.4 as amended by b.deo SRI-1203); its header comment is the full API. `tests/reload.test.ts` is the reference.

| Part | What it gives a test |
|------|----------------------|
| Temp config dir | `h.dir` under one fresh `mkdtempSync` root (`h.root`, real-path resolved), holding only the SR-8.1 files (as amended by b.deo SRI-401) named in `h.paths` (`config`, `pending`, `apply`, `lastApplied`, from `reloadFilePaths`); `h.configDirEntries()` shows exactly what was left there. `h.home` is a temp home for `~` in a test-written config. |
| Personas and files | `h.persona(name, overrides?)` is a file-form persona whose credentials file and working directory sit under the root; nothing exists until the test calls `h.writeCredentials` (a fresh sentinel-bearing token set on each call, so a second call rotates it; `h.tokens(name)` is the latest; a persona whose first credentials are written after a run was built, as for an addition, is registered then with each live run's Slack stubs, scripted by that run's `opts.slack[name]`; a rotation of a persona a live run already routes registers the new pair with that run as another credential set of it, unscripted, so a reconnect builds its clients there while the old set keeps serving), `h.writeCredentialsContent`, `h.makeWorkingDirectory` or `h.materialize`. `h.writeConfig` / `h.writeRecord` write the same input as byte-identical files; the `…Bytes` forms write raw bytes; `h.remove` and `h.replaceWithDirectory` make a file missing or unreadable. For an added persona that cannot come up, `h.preparePersona(name, { credentials?, workingDirectory? }, overrides?)` is `h.persona(name, overrides)` with its files left in the stated states: credentials `valid` (the default, `h.writeCredentials`), `missing`, `invalid` (parseable, no `bot_token`) or `unreadable` (a directory); working directory `present` (the default), `missing` or `file` (a plain file). Singly, `h.deleteWorkingDirectory(persona)` and `h.makeWorkingDirectoryAFile(persona)`. The controller judges these with its real checks, never canned outcomes. Every helper refuses a path outside the root. |
| Edits between ticks | Config: `h.writeConfig` / `h.writeConfigBytes`, `h.deleteConfig()`, `h.replaceWithDirectory(h.paths.config)`. Credentials: `h.writeCredentials` rotates to a new valid set, and `h.rotateCredentials(persona, { slack? })` does the same, returns `{ tokens, label }` and scripts the new set's stub in every live run with `slack` (unscripted, the new tokens are accepted with an identity of their own, another bot user ID and bot ID); every set written has a harness label, `credentials-<n>` (`h.credentialsLabel(name)` is the latest), `h.writeCredentialsContent` writes invalid content, `h.deleteCredentials(persona)`, `h.makeCredentialsUnreadable(persona)` (a directory at the path) and `h.makeFifo(path)` (guard with `mkfifoAvailable()`). `h.saveCredentials(persona)` returns a function that restores the file's exact bytes (or its absence, or the unreadable directory) and its token set, to revert an edit. `h.readCredentialsBytes(persona)` reads the file; `h.credentialsDigestOf(persona)` is the production reader's digest or marker for the file as it stands, to compare with `run.bringUps.credentialsDigest(key)`, never to print. |
| The pending file | `h.pendingExists()`, `h.readPending()` / `h.readPendingText()` (undefined unless a regular file), `h.pendingFingerprint()` (through `parsePendingFingerprint`) and `h.writePendingBytes(b)` for a leftover or hand-placed file. The preview in it: `h.pendingBody()` is the text after the two fingerprint lines and the blank line (found through `parsePendingFingerprint` and `composePendingFile`, not by re-parsing the layout), without the final newline, so it equals `renderPreview(plan)`; `h.pendingLines()` is its lines, `h.pendingHeader()` the first (the counted header, the `no effective change` header or the `INVALID:` line) and `h.pendingDestructiveLines()` those starting `DESTRUCTIVE:`. Each is undefined when the file is absent, not a regular file or not in the pending layout. |
| Write and delete failure seams | `h.failWrites({ step?, code?, call? })` makes the controller's `durableWriteFileSync` throw an errno-style error at that `DurableWriteFs` step (default `openSync`, `EIO`) until `h.clearWriteFailure()`: every call of the step, or with `call` (1-based, counted from `failWrites`) only that one, e.g. `{ step: 'fsyncSync', call: 2 }` for the directory fsync after the rename. `h.failRemoves({ step?, code?, call? })` / `h.clearRemoveFailure()` do the same for the controller's `durableUnlinkSync` (default `unlinkSync`; `{ step: 'fsyncSync' }` fails the directory fsync after the unlink). `h.failChannelDeliveryWrites({ step?, code?, call? })` / `h.clearChannelDeliveryWriteFailure()` take `failWrites`' options and fail only the stored-choice store's writes (b.deo SRI-1203); `h.failWrites` never fails a stored-choice write and does not count its calls. The three seams count calls separately. |
| Confirmations | `h.confirm()` is the operator's gesture exactly: one rename of `h.paths.pending` to `h.paths.apply` with no content change (AC 73); it throws when there is no pending file. `run.confirmPending()` is the whole confirmation of a change already on disk: a tick that must write the pending file (it throws otherwise), the rename, then the applying tick, returned unawaited as `{ applying }` so a test can hold a lifecycle call or advance `run.clock` while it runs. `run.beforeApplySteps(hook)` runs `hook` once at the next apply's step 1, after the applied set was swapped and before step 2, for example to retarget a symlink so a credentials real path collides after the candidate was validated (b.av2 SR-1.4, apply part). `h.writeApplyBytes(b)` places any bytes at the apply path (an older pending copy kept with `h.readPending()` for a stale gesture, or garbage for a malformed one); `h.applyExists()` and `h.readApply()` show what is there. A confirmation that cannot be deleted is `h.failRemoves()`, never file modes. |
| Retired-key store | Every run loads one store over `h.stateDir` (`loadRetiredKeyStore`, as `main()` loads its one store) and hands it to the controller as `retiredKeys`; `run.retiredKeys` is that store (to seed a key held only in memory, record a batch through it with the writer failing). It writes and deletes through the run's own writer and delete, so its writes are in `run.writes`, its deletes in `run.removes`, and `h.failWrites` / `h.failRemoves` count its calls with the controller's: an apply that records makes the retired-key write before the last-applied write, so on `h.failWrites` the last-applied write's `openSync` is the third and its directory `fsyncSync` the fourth. In `run.writes` a stored-choice write may come first (an unwritten drop, b.deo SRI-408) and a drop write may follow the last-applied write; the Write and delete failure seams row says which seam fails and counts them. Its lines go to `run.logs`, its times come from `run.clock`, `run.captured()` holds the record while it exists. The record's path is `retiredKeysPath(h.stateDir)`, also `h.retiredKeysFile`; `h.retiredKeysWrite(ok?)` and `h.lastAppliedWrite(ok?)` build the writer calls on it and on the last-applied record (`ok` true by default), as `run.writes` holds them, so a case asserts the write order with them. Seed and read it with `tests/test-helpers/retired-keys.ts`. A later run (a restart) loads a new store from the disk, and a record it cannot load makes `h.build` throw. A `realLaunch` run also installs that same store for the session manager (`setRetiredKeyStore`, as `main()` does right after the configured-persona query, before its start pass; b.jg5 SRJ-805; b.jg5 SRJ-806 as amended by b.deo SRI-407), so a launch reads the keys apply step 1 recorded, a reuse that succeeds for a recorded key sets its mark through it, and an own-row read clears through it; `run.stop()` resets the install when it is the run's own, `h.cleanup()` always does, and a run without `realLaunch` installs none. |
| Stored-choice store | Every run loads one stored-choice store over `h.stateDir` (`loadChannelDeliveryAtStart`) in `run.resolveStart()` once the start resolves to an applied configuration, as `main()` loads its one store: after the resolution and before the start bring-up pass, in both modes and in dry run, with the start rules over the applied personas and the run's retired-key store (b.deo SRI-403, SRI-407, SRI-1203). A refused start loads none. The controller gets it as `channelDelivery`, an accessor with `main()`'s guard (it throws when an apply runs before the load). `run.channelDelivery` is the store; reading it before the start resolved to an applied configuration, or after a refused start, throws. Its writes take the run's writer path, so they are in `run.writes` in order with the record writes, `opts.beforeWrite` and `opts.stopBeforeWrite` see them, `run.captured()` holds the file while it exists and `h.serverSideFiles` sweeps it; their failures come from the stored-choice seam (see the Write and delete failure seams row). Its deletes go through the run's delete (`run.removes`, `h.failRemoves`); its lines go to `run.logs` and its times come from `run.clock`. The file's path is `channelDeliveryPath(h.stateDir)`, also `h.channelDeliveryFile`; `h.channelDeliveryWrite(ok?)` builds the writer call on it as `run.writes` holds it. `h.readChannelDelivery()` reads the record through the module's parser (`null` when there is no file). Seed a valid record with `writeChannelDeliveryRecord(h.stateDir, entries)` and invalid or unreadable bytes with `h.writeChannelDeliveryBytes(bytes)`, which writes exactly those bytes outside every seam and record. A later run over the same directories (a restart) loads a fresh store from the disk, so its retiring keys and unwritten drops start empty. |
| Old-life holds | Every run builds one hold set, empty (`createOldLifeHoldSet`, its lines to `run.logs`), as `main()` builds its one set beside the store, and hands it to the controller as `oldLifeHolds`, so a confirmed apply's step 1 begins its holds in it once the last-applied rewrite succeeded (b.jg5 SRJ-809). A `realLaunch` run also installs that same set for the session manager (`setOldLifeHolds`, beside the store, as `main()` does), so its reads end or re-point a hold, a recorded key's reuse that begins its new life ends one, and the teardown's kill marks one kill-failed; `run.stop()` resets the install when it is the run's own, `h.cleanup()` always does, and a run without `realLaunch` installs none. With `realLaunch` the run also binds what a hold refuses, as `main()` does: the old-life gate holds a persona back in the real `spawnForPersona`, `run.admitSession` passes the session manager's held-directory query (`oldLifeHeldDirectory`), and right after the set's install (so after the session manager's own end observer) the end-retry observer (`createOldLifeHoldEndRetry` over the run's retry timers' `runNow`, cause `held-for-old-life`, label `old-life-hold-ended`) is registered on it, so a hold's end retries each applied, unlatched waiting persona at once; `run.stop()` removes it. `run.oldLifeHolds` (`ReloadOldLifeHoldsView`) reads it: `holdOf`, `holdsOnDirectory`, `holdsWaitedOnBy`, `waitsOnKillFailed`, `snapshot`. Holds live in server memory: a later run over the same directories (a restart) starts with a new, empty set, and the harness runs no start sweep to rebuild one; a case about the rebuild uses the recovery harness's `h.startSweep`, its record seeded through `tests/test-helpers/retired-keys.ts`. |
| A stop between writes | `opts.stopBeforeWrite` names a path (e.g. `h.paths.lastApplied`, to stop between apply step 1's retired-key write and its last-applied write, or `h.channelDeliveryFile`, to stop before a stored-choice write). Just before the run's writer is first called for it, the harness keeps an image of every regular file under the configuration and state directories, and `run.stop()` puts that image back, so the next run (a restart) sees the disk as it stood then. The write and the rest of the run go on and the controller sees no failure, so no restore runs. `run.stoppedBeforeWrite` says the point was reached; `h.build` throws until such a run is stopped. |
| Oversized files | `h.writeOversized(path, { prefix?, size? })` writes a file over the 64 KiB read cap (`MAX_RELOAD_FILE_BYTES + 1` bytes by default): `prefix`, then sentinel-bearing fake-token text, so a leak of its content fails `assertNoLeak`. Use it for any config, record, pending, apply or credentials path. |
| A run | `h.build(opts?)`, or `h.start(opts?)` (build, `resolveStart`, which loads the run's stored-choice store when the start applied, then the start bring-up when the start applied), is one server start with its own real connection manager over stub Slack (each persona's latest tokens), real bring-up controller (`run.bringUps`) and fake clock (`run.clock`). The controller's `checkConfigDir` resolves against the harness's temp home (`checkPersonaConfigDir(persona, { home })`), or is `checkLaunchConfigDir` over the harness's spawn home with a real launch, never the process home. `h.start` arms no detection tick; `h.startDetecting(opts?)` is `h.start` then `run.startDetection()`, as `main()` does, and throws when nothing was armed (call `run.startDetection()` yourself to test a refused or early call). `opts.slack` scripts a persona's stub by name: `SLACK_UNREACHABLE` leaves it `retrying` however far the clock advances and `SLACK_AUTH_REJECTED` leaves it `broken`, each holding the digest of the file it read. `opts.dryRun` reaches the connection manager, the bring-up controller and the reload controller as a flag, never through `process.env`. `opts.bringUpFs` overrides the bring-up file-system seam, `opts.configFs` passes a `PersonaConfigFs` override to the controller's record, config and pending-file reads, and `opts.credentialsFs` a `CredentialsFs` override to the detection tick's credentials reads. `opts.heldCredentialsDigest(key, held)` wraps the controller's held-digest lookup (`held` is the default, `run.bringUps.credentialsDigest`), for example to throw and so fail a detection check. The controller's `bringUpState` is bound to `run.bringUps.state`, as `server.ts` binds it; `opts.bringUpState(key, state)` wraps it the same way, for example to throw so the preview leaves that fact out. The tick's checks of an added persona are the controller's own (the real credentials content check over the bytes the tick read, none in dry run, `checkPersonaWorkingDirectory` over the temp directory, always on the real file system, and the default `checkPersonaConfigDir` against `h.home`). The bring-up controller gets the manager's `bringUp`, `status` and `stop`, as `server.ts` wires it, so a `claude_config_dir` hold that starts later closes the persona's connection. `opts.tickDriver` replaces the manual driver (see the rules). A confirmed apply: without `opts.applySteps` the controller runs its default fan-out over the recorder (step 2 tears down each removed persona and the old half of each destructive modify, step 3 updates each persona modified in place, step 4 reconnects each persona whose credentials changed and that is not credentials-broken, step 5 refreshes the template once when the plan's config directories changed, and step 6 brings up each added persona and the new half of each destructive modify and, as a recovery, each credentials-changed persona step 4 did not reconnect; a next-launch change gets no record); `opts.applySteps` replaces all of steps 2–6 (`ApplyStepSlots`), for testing the step runner itself. `opts.realLifecycle` binds the real composition behind the recorder's `teardown`, `updateInPlace`, `reconnectCredentials`, `refreshTemplate` and `bringUp` (a recovery included; `run.composition`, see the Real composition row), and hands the bring-up controller and the connection manager its serializer, and the controller the live applied set, as `server.ts` does (so a b.ujn mark's socket close waits for the persona's turn); `opts.agentDirector` passes `StubClientOptions` (e.g. `killError`) to its agent-director stub. `run.appliedConfigs` holds every configuration the controller's `onApplied` was told, in order (`opts.onApplied` is called too); `run.appliedKeys()` is the controller's applied persona keys now; `run.serverConfig()` is `server.ts`'s `personaConfig` (`configInEffect`, which takes the confirmed persona set and the confirmed `allow_invited_channels` switch and keeps every other server-wide setting of the start-time config), which every consumer and real launch reads, so a confirmed change to any other server-wide setting shows in `run.appliedConfigs` and `h.readRecord()` but not there until a later `h.start()` over the same directories (the next server start). `opts.beforeWrite(path)` runs before every writer call, for example to see whether `h.paths.apply` still exists when the record is written. |
| Manual tick driver | `run.ticks` (`createManualTickDriver`): nothing runs until the test calls `tick()` or `ticks(n)`, each awaited until the pass settles; `tick` rejects when nothing is armed. `armed`, `startCalls`, `stopCalls` and `ticksRun` show how the controller used it. |
| Lifecycle recorder | `run.lifecycle` (`ReloadLifecycleRecorder`) is the controller's `ReloadLifecycleOps`. Bring-ups and launches go through the real bring-up controller (a launch is only recorded); teardowns and in-place updates are recorded, an in-place update's record (`update-in-place`) with the changed `settings` in the controller's order. A `reconnect` runs the real controller's `changeCredentials` over the manager (the real reconnect: new connection first, the 10 s `start()` bound and the SR-3.2 retries on `run.clock`), and its record gets how its first attempt settled (`change`). A recovery bring-up's record has `recovery: true`; the stand-in cancels the persona's bring-up, stops its connection and brings it up afresh. Neither stand-in ever forgets a cached DM conversation, so a reconnect or recovery of a persona that has had a `dm` destination in the run, or a recovery of a persona no longer credentials-broken when it runs (the lifecycle applies that as a reconnect), needs `opts.realLifecycle`: without it the stand-in refuses the call (it rejects), records the refusal, and `h.cleanup()` throws it, naming the persona. A launch's record carries `via`: `start`, `retry` (a bring-up retry), `apply`, `restart` (`run.relaunch`) or `unavailable-retry` (a retry of the run's retry timers, `opts.realLaunch` only; see the Real launch path row). Read `records`, `of(op)`, `keys(op)`, `outcome(key)`, `classes(key)` and `startPasses`; `holdStartPass()` holds the next start pass open after its bring-ups and launches: call the release it returns to let the pass return, or `release.fail(err)` to make the pass throw `err`. Apply-time calls: `hold(op, key)` (`op` is `teardown`, `update-in-place`, `reconnect` or `bring-up`) holds that persona's next call after it is recorded but before its body runs; `entered` resolves when it arrives, `release()` runs the body and `fail(err)` makes it reject without running it (call `fail` early to reject at once). `timeline` lists every apply-time teardown, in-place update, reconnect and bring-up as `{op, key, phase}` (`start`, then `settled` or `rejected`) across personas and steps, so step order is asserted without timing; start-pass bring-ups are not in it. Step 5 is a `template-refresh` record under the key `TEMPLATE_REFRESH_KEY` (with the real composition, its `refresh` says how it settled; the stand-in calls nothing); `hold('template-refresh')` holds it the same way, and `applyTimeline` is `timeline` with step 5 in its place, for step order across all six steps. The teardown stand-in refuses the old half of a destructive modify of a persona that has had a `dm` destination (its new half would post through a DM conversation only the lifecycle forgets), like the other stand-in refusals. |
| Latch and notice episodes | Every run builds, as `main()` does, one set of notice episodes (`createPersonaEpisodes` on `run.clock`, posting through the run's notifier) and one latch (`createConflictLatch`, its lines to `run.logs`) whose CONFLICT notice is bound to those episodes (`bindConflictNotice`). The holds `main()` binds before the notice are not bound: a run has no UNAVAILABLE retry controller, tmux-unresponsive condition or unclassified-error episode. `run.latch` (`ReloadLatchView`: `isLatched(key)`, `record(key)`) reads the latch, and `run.episodeNotices` is every post of the episodes as `{ key, text }`, in order (also in `run.captured()`). With `opts.realLaunch` the latch is installed in the session manager (`setConflictLatch`, uninstalled by `h.cleanup()`), and beside it, as `main()` does, the configured-persona query over the run's live applied configuration (`setConfiguredPersonaQuery`, reset by `h.cleanup()` with `_resetConfiguredPersonaQuery()`), so a CONFLICT at a ladder spawn or `resume` latches the persona (its launch record's `action` is `latched`, with one CONFLICT notice to its destination) and a latched persona's launch makes no agent-director call. After the latch, as `main()` does, a `realLaunch` run builds and installs its live-row sequence registry (b.jg5 SRJ-706: `createLiveRowSequenceRegistry` over the session manager's `buildLiveRowSequenceDeps` on the run's clock and `run.serverConfig()`, each sequence in its own detached recovery attempt, its retry arm arming nothing as the run has no retry controller; `setLiveRowSequenceRegistry`): `run.startSequence(name, lastReadState?)` starts the persona's sequence through the session manager's start entry (from step 1, its launch kept, the recovery context; throwing unless it answered `started`) and answers its outcome promise, `run.sequenceRunning(name)` reads the running query, and `run.composition.holdFindMissing()` holds its runs (the stub's `holdFindMissing`; the case releases every held call before cleanup), so a case can hold a sequence across an apply, and `run.composition.holdSpawns(shouldHold)` holds every `spawn` that reaches the stub whose instance ID passes `shouldHold` (the stub's `holdSpawns`, answering its `SpawnHold`; a spawn the row table answers with a collision never reaches the stub, so it is never held; the case releases every held spawn before cleanup), so a case can hold a reuse mid-call (b.jg5 SRJ-806 as amended by b.deo SRI-407); `run.stop()` closes the registry, as `main()`'s shutdown does, and `h.cleanup()` removes it. The latch is per run: a later run (a server restart) or another harness starts with none. The `ErrInvalidFlags` hold (b.jg5 SRJ-207, SRJ-1008): every run builds, as `main()` does, one hold (`createInvalidFlagsHold`, its lines to `run.logs`) with its set reaction bound to the run's episodes (`bindInvalidFlagsHoldSetReaction`: a new hold posts the alert once through the run's notifier, in `run.episodeNotices`; its retry-timer stop stops nothing, as a run has no retry controller). A `realLaunch` run installs it in the session manager beside the latch (`setInvalidFlagsHold`), so a reuse spawn's `ErrInvalidFlags` holds the persona (its launch record's `action` is `held`; no version re-check is installed, so the re-check answers not running and the hold begins under no version) and a held persona's launch makes no agent-director call. The composition's `forgetInvalidFlagsHold` is the hold's `forget`, right after the latch's. `run.invalidFlagsHold` (`ReloadInvalidFlagsHoldView`: `isHeld(key)`, `beganUnder(key)`) reads it. The hold is per run, like the latch; `h.cleanup()` removes the install. |
| Consumers and post-apply drivers | Every run wires the real consumers of the applied settings as `server.ts` wires them, each reading the controller's applied configuration at each use: the routing (`createPersonaRouting`) behind the real event router on the connection manager's `onEvent`, and one destination resolver, destination hold and notifier (built by `makeNotifierStack`, the hold on that stack's own fake clock, never `run.clock`), whose up-flush listener joins the bring-up controller on `onStatus`. None of them is exposed on the run; reach them only through the drivers. Drive the next event, call or post after an apply with: `run.registerSession(name)` (registers the persona's MCP session in the real registry; `run.session(name)` is its session now, compared by identity to show it was kept); `run.deliver(name, event, envelope?)` (delivers a Slack event on the persona's socket stub, through the event router and routing; `envelope`'s overrides go to the stub's `deliver` unchanged, e.g. an `ENVELOPE_FLAG_FORMS` form, and none gives `false`) and `run.deliveries(name)` (queued observations projected as `chat_id`, `via` and `content`); `run.notice(name, text)` (raises a notice through the real notifier; read its Slack calls on `run.stub(name).callLog`); `run.callTool(name, tool, args, session?)` (calls an MCP tool as the persona's instance over `createSessionServer` with the real tool handlers, answering `isError` and `text`; needs `run.registerSession` first, and refuses every file). `session` is an entry kept from `run.session(name)`: the call goes through a client over that entry even after the registry dropped it or a newer registration replaced it (b.deo SRI-502), and it throws when the entry is matched to another key; there is one client per entry, closed by `run.stop()`. The routing and the tool deps are bound to the run's store and mode as `server.ts` binds them (b.deo SRI-1203): the routing's `getChannelDelivery` reads the run's stored-choice store at each event, and the tools read, at each call, `getChannelMode` (`channelModeOf(run.serverConfig())`), that same store, the routing's `heardChannels` and the applied personas (`getAppliedPersonas`), with `log` into `run.logs`. Before the store is loaded, and after a refused start, both read none, so no stored choice applies and `set_channel_delivery` refuses. The notifier's destination resolver reads the switch from the configuration in effect at each attempt (`makeNotifierStack`'s `getPersonaConfig`). Queue observations prove acceptance, not typing or transcript confirmation. Preparation resolves names after acknowledgement and adds no ack reaction. `run.noticeClock` is the destination hold's fake clock: advance it to drive the retries of a held notice. |
| Credential sets and sockets | `run.credentialsStub(name, label?)` is a credential set's stub in this run (default the latest; the set the run was built with is also `run.stub(name)`), and `run.currentStub(name)` the one whose socket connected last, the set serving the persona now. `run.socketActivity(name)` is the persona's socket clients' events in order, labelled by set (`'connected credentials-2'`, `'discarded credentials-1'`, …), which tells "new opened, then old closed" apart; the full record is `run.slack.activityOf(key)`. The current identity is `run.connections.manager.identity(key)`. |
| Not-up personas | `run.isUp(name)` is `server.ts`'s up check (`createPersonaUpPredicate`), `run.admitSession(name)` its MCP admission (`decideSessionAdmission`: registers when admitted, else one refusal line; with `opts.realLaunch` it answers `held` for a working directory an old life holds, b.jg5 SRJ-810, SRJ-1505) and `run.bringUps.state(key)` the outcome. To break a running persona: `run.revokeBotToken(name, { error?, credentials?, queues? })` puts `token_revoked` (or `error`) at the front of every Web API queue of its current stub, so the first call any consumer makes marks it credentials-broken (bug b.ujn), and returns a function that removes the entries still queued; `run.refuseReopen(name, error?)` drops its socket with a refused reopen queued and resolves once it is broken. `run.linesOf(name)` is every log line naming the persona. |
| Token environment | `h.poisonTokenEnvironment()` sets `SLACK_BOT_TOKEN` and `SLACK_APP_TOKEN` to distinct sentinel-bearing fakes and records every read of either through `process.env` in `reads`, to show nothing uses the environment (AC 19, AC 22). One at a time; `restore()` puts both back, and `h.cleanup()` calls it. |
| Captures | `run.logs` (one `[slack]` stream, in order, with the tool deps' lines, such as `persona-channel-delivery-set`, in every run) and `run.logsOf(label)` (its lines starting `[slack] <label>: `, a class such as `RELOAD_PREVIEW` or `'reload'` for the unclassed failure lines) and `run.invalidLines()` (`logsOf(RELOAD_INVALID)`); `run.tickCredentialsReads` (every credentials path the detection tick opened, empty for a whole dry run) and `run.tickDirectoryChecks` (every working directory the tick's added-persona check stat'd), which are not part of `since`, so slice them by length around a stretch of ticks; `run.writes` (every writer call with its path and `ok`, the stored-choice store's included); `run.removes` (every durable delete as `{path, ok, removed, unsynced}`: `ok` means the file is gone after the call, `removed` whether this call removed it (undefined when the unlink failed), `unsynced` that it was removed but the directory sync failed); `run.pendingWrites()` / `run.pendingRemoves()` (those on `h.paths.pending`); `run.slackCalls()`, `run.slackPosts()`; `run.episodeNotices`; and `run.captured(extra?)`, which gathers all of them plus every file the run's writer wrote that still exists (as `writtenFile`) for `assertNoLeak`. |
| Preview emissions | One logged preview spans several `reload-preview` lines; its header starts `PREVIEW_EMISSION_START` (`[slack] reload-preview: ` then `PENDING_PREVIEW_TITLE`). `run.previewEmissions()` groups `run.logs` into emissions (a header and the `reload-preview` lines right after it), `run.previewEmissionCount()` counts them, `run.lastPreviewEmission()` is the latest one's full log lines and `run.lastPreviewText()` the same as body lines (prefix removed, and the header's pending-file suffix), which equals `h.pendingLines()` when the file was written. The exported `previewEmissions(lines)` and `previewEmissionText(emission, pendingFile)` do the same over any slice, such as `run.since(cp).logs`; a header whose write failed keeps its text, so assert the missing suffix on the full log line. A `reload-invalid` line is not an emission. |
